● Three agents running. Progress report while they work:

  Closed and committed since the last report

  - G8 — cross-check widened ledger-wide (b6e2a1d). supported_kinds() went from
  the 6 Proven kinds to all 20, with a per-disposition evidence rule:
  Proven/ConfigPredicate/ConfirmOnly need a passing fixture, FailClosed needs a
  refusal witness instead. This surfaced a real overclaim: MprGroupOverwrite
  (FailClosed) was reporting Covered purely because its sibling MprGroupAppend
  shares the construct id "MPR features/groups" — the refusal was never
  exercised. Fixed and pinned.
  - G9 — construct vocabularies aligned. 4 missing rows added upstream
  (sillsdev/machine#465); Unmappable is now zero. The 12 orphan rows got
  documented reasons, not invented characteristics.
  - New gate I added on review: G8 makes a FailClosed row's Covered verdict rest
  entirely on a hand-written citation string, and nothing checked that string
  resolved. Three tests now verify cited files and #[test] functions exist —
  proven non-vacuous both ways (renaming the fn fails 1-of-30; renaming the file
  fails two gates).
  - Both NEEDS-DECISION rows resolved → PROVABLE, build, no carve-out (cecc936).
  Each blocker rested on a premise that didn't survive checking the code:
  unbounded quantifiers are unattempted, not infeasible (foma has a native exact
  ^>N/*, and max=-1 is the loader's default, so it's a hole on the commonest
  shape); RTL metathesis is not from scratch (replace.rs's mirror-and-reverse
  machinery transfers directly).
  - Circumfix census done — row 10's blocking task. The mechanism is
  allomorph-complete; all three gaps are candidate selection, all fail
  over-refusing. C1 (rule_role reads allomorph 0 only) is
  order-of-declaration-dependent and the highest priority.
  - G11 verdict: 2 was a faithful default, but C# exposes MaxStemCount as a
  settable property and its own tests raise it to 3 — the gap was hardcoding,
  now a builder. All gates observed green including f3_parity (3/3, ~20 min).

  Two things needing your call

  1. machine/conformance/languages/ still contains actual-language data. The
  delanguaging pass renamed the directories only. The data is real: nui/niu
  "coconut", sumulat/SULAT with the -um- infix, keadilan, Swahili-shaped and
  Semitic-root-shaped forms. That's 8 fixtures / 217 words. I deliberately did
  not touch it — it's SIL's upstream suite on a shared branch, rewriting it is
  outward-facing, and it would mean re-deriving all 217 ground-truth signatures
  that the 15+1 baseline rests on. Did the synthetic-only rule mean to cover the
  pre-existing upstream suite, or only our own fixtures? (Ours are genuinely
  synthetic — verified.)
  2. The flip is close. Only 4 rows blocked it, and the cause is mundane: three
  fixtures tag characteristic names where a constructs.txt row id is required,
  so those tags have been contributing nothing, silently. An agent is fixing
  that plus adding the gate that makes an unknown tag fail loudly. If that lands
  clean, the build-breaking flip is a one-line change I'll make.

          +normalized spelling, applied at `render_slots` only, leaving `

          +s the oracle matches natural classes by feature lanes, not cha

● Agent(Fix the unmarked-guess FFI overclaim) Sonnet 5
  ⎿  Backgrounded agent (↓ to manage · ctrl+o to expand)

  Committed ab2f915 (ctrl+o to expand)

● Two significant findings while the agents work, both now documented and
  queued:

  1. An overclaim in shipping code — the most serious thing found this session.
  pg_lexicon::analysis retries via the guesser unconditionally on any total miss
  (analysis.rs:127-129, no switch). The guessed fact is carried correctly all
  the way up — and then discarded at the FFI boundary, because
  hc_parse_word/hc_parse_batch's wire format has no guessed field. So those two
  symbols have been handing callers guessed analyses byte-indistinguishable from
  confirmed ones. That's the one invariant broken in shipping code, not in a
  plan.

  I decided the fix rather than adding a bit to the existing format (which would
  break every native decoder): those two symbols become guess-off, since they
  cannot mark what they return. Callers wanting guessing use the _opts symbols
  added this session, whose format carries an explicit guessed byte. Existing
  callers lose results they were silently being handed — the right direction,
  since a dropped guess is a recoverable disappointment and an unmarked guess is
  a false claim. An agent is implementing it, including an encoder-level guard
  so a future caller can't reintroduce it.

  2. A recall-loss risk from two individually-correct changes interacting.
  reversed_slots is a shallow .iter().rev() that doesn't recurse into a
  Slot::Repeat's children, so the RTL mirror rule isn't the reverse of the
  original when a quantifier group's children aren't palindromic. It's
  reachable: is_fully_supported_shape gates only on RewriteMode, and
  pattern_slots has accepted bounded quantifiers since the bounded-quantifier
  change — so the predicate now admits these at ConfirmOnly, which guards
  against over-generation but not omission.

  I have not reproduced this, and I'm flagging it as structural rather than
  measured on purpose — the existing RTL-quantifier fixture has a single-child
  quantifier, trivially palindromic, so it passes and doesn't detect it. First
  task of the fix is authoring a multi-child fixture and confirming the miss
  before touching any code.

  Also landed: the MultiTable design (tasks.md 4.4). Working it through showed
  the fix the plan named — disjoint token ranges — is the wrong one: the failure
  direction is a false negative (a rule silently not firing on the other
  table's material, unrecoverable under propose-and-confirm), and range
  separation would entrench it. Recommended instead: cross-table representation
  aliasing at render time. Recall-safe by construction, no frozen-model change,
  and it makes the row smaller in scope than the plan assumed, not larger.

  Three agents running: G4+G5 (trace bookends + gate order), the 4 flip-blocking
  coverage rows, and the FFI overclaim fix. I'll run the full debug+release
  workspace myself once the tree is quiescent — the concurrent edits have been
  making agents' release runs flaky, and one trace-golden failure is expected
  from the tracing work in flight.

● I stopped short of the flip, and I want to be direct about why, since it's the
  headline item.

  The cross-check now reports 20 rows / 20 Covered / 0 Uncovered / 0 Unmappable.
  The four blocking rows weren't missing evidence at all — their exercises:
  tags were characteristic names where a constructs.txt row id is required, so
  they credited nothing, silently. constructs.txt's own header explains how that
  went unnoticed: the harness treats an unknown tag as "a soft warning… never a
  hard error." Fixed, plus a gate that makes an unknown tag fail loudly, proven
  non-vacuous.

  But I'm not flipping on that number, and the reason isn't the number. Four row
  ids are each mapped by two characteristics, because constructs.txt is the
  coarser upstream vocabulary. Byte-for-byte set matching can't distinguish
  "this fixture exercises the finer construct" from "its coarser sibling tags
  the same row." That's the same defect G8 just fixed for MprGroupOverwrite —
  scoped to the FailClosed split, so the general case survived.

  I hand-verified all four by reading the covering grammars, not the aggregate:
  polysynthetic-stratal-derivation-chain really does declare
  morphologicalRuleOrder="unordered"; suffixing-vowel-harmony really has an
  empty-PhoneticInput epenthesis rule; gelobt/gelobth really are circumfixes. So
  20/20 is true. It's just not mechanically true — delete the unordered strata
  and the row keeps reporting Covered off its sibling, with nothing failing.

  That matters most for circumfix, where I'd be lighting a green build-breaking
  gate directly beside a census I wrote hours ago documenting three real
  circumfix gaps. A gate whose green light can decay into a false claim is worse
  than an advisory report, because the green light is what gets cited. So:
  structural witness gate first (grammar shape cross-checked against tag,
  shared-id list computed rather than hardcoded), then flip. An agent is
  building it.

  I also want to flag a distinction the docs now state explicitly, because it's
  easy to over-read "20/20": row-level coverage and configuration-level
  completeness are different questions. Ten rows are ConfigPredicate, three
  ConfirmOnly, and several have open configuration splits inside them —
  circumfix C1/C3, the unbounded quantifier, RTL metathesis, MultiTable 4.4b.
  Covered means "evidenced at its own disposition," never "proven admissible."

  Three agents running: G4+G5 (trace bookends + gate order), the FFI
  unmarked-guess fix, and the witness gate. Still open for you: whether the
  synthetic-only rule was meant to cover SIL's pre-existing
  machine/conformance/languages/ suite, which still holds real-language data
  under its delanguaged directory names.

● One correction worth stating plainly: my previous commit message described the
  --guess CLI flag, but I'd only staged the FFI crates. That's now committed as
  its own change, and the message says so.

  Landed since the last report

  - The overclaim is closed. pg_lexicon's guesser retry is now explicit opt-in
  defaulting off; hc_parse_word/hc_parse_batch pass false, so a total miss stays
  a total miss instead of returning an unmarked guess. Plus defence in depth:
  the old format's encoder now filters guessed analyses, so a future caller
  flipping the switch can't silently reintroduce it. Filter rather than assert —
  panicking at an extern "C" boundary turns a condition the function can fix
  itself into an availability incident for the host.
  - I caught and fixed a silent regression the agent left behind. Flipping
  analyze_word's default kept the signature, so pg-wasm still compiled while
  quietly ceasing to guess — and its own doc says guessing there is deliberate
  ("part of what the demo is for, not a fallback to hide"). Both call sites now
  opt in explicitly. This is the step that turns a default flip from a silent
  regression into a reviewed one; the agent reported it rather than fixing it,
  which was correct scoping on its part.
  - G3's guesser surface (CLI flag, additive FFI symbols with a real guessed
  byte, ABI 2→3, fixture with engine-derived ground truth) and G11's 
  max_stem_count verdict — 2 was a faithful default, but C# exposes it as a
  settable property and raises it to 3 in its own tests, so the gap was the
  hardcoding.

         +tch_opts` as additive FFI symbols with                         
      72 +  their own magic carrying an explicit `guessed` byte at word *
         +and* analysis level; ABI 2 → 3; fixture                        
      73 +  `conformance-staging/edge-cases/guesser-pattern-root-fallback
         +` with engine-derived ground truth. The                        
      74 +  adjacent overclaim found while building it (see "OVERCLAIM in
         + shipping code" below) is also closed:                         
      75 +  the `pg_lexicon` retry is now explicit opt-in defaulting off,
         + the two old symbols pass `false`, and                         
      76 +  the old format's encoder additionally *filters* guessed analy
         +ses so a future caller cannot                                  
      77 +  reintroduce it. `pg-wasm` opts back in explicitly at both cal
         +l sites — the default flip would                               
      78 +  otherwise have silently stopped the demo guessing, which its 
         +own doc says is deliberate.                                    
      79 +- **§3 item 2 (analysis-side tracing) — CLOSED.** All five prev
         +iously-unwired events now fire:                                
      80 +  `begin_unapply_stratum` (`stratum.rs::analyze`), `end_unapply
         +_stratum` at both C# exits,                                    
      81 +  `begin_unapply_template`, `end_unapply_template`, and `lexica
         +l_lookup` at both the real-lexicon and                         
      82 +  guesser sites. The four C# `end_unapply_template` line-pairs 
         +collapse onto two Rust call sites                              
      83 +  because C# has `#if SINGLE_THREADED` and `Parallel.ForEach` i
         +mplementations of `ApplySlots` each                            
      84 +  firing the same two logical events — a structural fact, not u
         +nder-wiring.

         +ather than accepted.** The syn-FS gate                         
      86 +  moved to C#'s position (last) in both `synth_affix` and `synt
         +h_affix_cached`. Outcome invariance                            
      87 +  verified by reading: the three intervening gates read only `r
         +ule`/`word.flags`/`word.mpr`/the root                          
      88 +  allomorph's stem name, never `word.syn_fs`, and `synth_syn_fs
         +` is pure. The analysis-side twin was                          
      89 +  checked and correctly left alone — it has only the one gate, 
         +so there is no order to fix.

● G6 is closed, and the measurement found something more important than the
  latencies.

  All three reference grammars are refused by the --engine=foma optimized path
  under default capability enforcement:

  ┌────────────┬───────────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────┐
  │  Grammar   │        Refusing predicate         │       Gap status       │
  ├────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┤
  │            │                                   │ PROVABLE — the         │
  │ Indonesian │ quantifier.bounded-expansion      │ unbounded quantifier I │
  │            │                                   │  resolved yesterday    │
  ├────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┤
  │ Amharic    │ mpr-group.overwrite-output        │ permanent carve-out    │
  ├────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┤
  │ Sena       │ compounding.non-recursive + the   │ one PROVABLE, one      │
  │            │ same MPR carve-out                │ permanent              │
  └────────────┴───────────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────┘

  Two things follow that I want to state directly, because they cut against how
  "20/20 Covered" reads:

  1. The cross-check reporting 20/20 does not mean the optimized path runs on 
  the reference grammars. It runs on none of them. Coverage asks whether each
  construct has evidence at its disposition; this asks whether a grammar clears
  the gate. Both are true at once, and neither implies the other.
  2. MprGroupOverwrite is permanently carved out and appears in two of the 
  three. So "the FST path runs all three reference grammars with enforcement on"
  is not reachable by design — only via the ADR 0005 override, stamped
  trust=unproven. That's the honest boundary, not a defect to fix.

  This also settles what the unbounded-quantifier decision left open. I'd argued
  it mattered because max=-1 is the DTD default and therefore probably common;
  it turns out to be load-bearing in a reference grammar, where it alone blocks
  Indonesian. That raises 4.5's priority from "coverage nicety" to "unblocks the
  optimized path on a real grammar."

  Oracle-path latency (the path explicitly not optimized): Indonesian p50 <1 ms
  / max 42 ms; Amharic p50 131 ms but p99 105 s, worst word 7.6 min; Sena worst
  10.3 min. Fine at the median, four to five orders of magnitude past the sub-10
  ms target at the tail — which is precisely why propose-and-confirm exists.
  Sena's run was killed at 5,365 of 7,121 words, so I've labelled those
  percentiles indicative, not final, rather than quoting them as complete.

  One optimized-path datapoint, force-compiled and labelled as such: Indonesian
  on foma is ~11× faster end-to-end (0.02 s vs 0.23 s), p95 1 ms vs 5 ms, with 
  byte-identical signatures for all 121 words. I deliberately did not
  force-compile Amharic or Sena — a latency number for a permanently-carved-out
  construct is underwritten by nothing and would invite exactly the over-reading
  the doc is trying to prevent.

  I also deferred the build-time column rather than half-filling it: the CLI
  measures compile_ms/morpher_build_ms but doesn't emit them, and the three
  grammars that matter are refused before compilation, so it would have exactly
  one honest row.

  Session state: working tree clean, 11 commits on main, debug 1222 / release
  1221 both zero-failure, conformance at 15+1, openspec 27/27. Still yours: the
  upstream real-language-data question, and the submodule re-point when PR #465
  merges (now build-breaking-relevant).

● Three agents running on the remaining implementable gaps. Here's the honest
  state of "all gaps."

  Closed this round (by deciding, not building): audit §3 item 3, the
  XmlLanguageWriter round-trip. Porting C#'s 1,321-line writer would build an
  exporter for a format we're retiring — the 2026-07-16 decisions sunset HC XML
  in PanGloss, and the grammar→external round-trip is already served by
  lcm-grammar v1 (sillsdev/liblcm#392) with a stronger two-implementation byte
  gate against pg-fwdata. Reclassified a permanent non-goal for that format,
  with the note that a legacy consumer would reopen it as its own change. I also
  put a banner on §3 saying it's historical: items 1/2/4/5/6/7 closed, item 3
  won't-do.

  In flight, all file-disjoint:
  - 4.5 unbounded quantifier + the RTL mirror bug (#32). 4.5 is now the
  highest-value item in the backlog — the benchmark showed it's the sole reason
  the foma path refuses Indonesian. Its acceptance test is exactly that:
  --engine=foma must run without --allow-unproven and match the default engine
  byte-for-byte on all 121 words. For #32 I told the agent to reproduce before 
  fixing, and that a well-argued "not reproduced" is the better outcome than a
  speculative fix.
  - 6.1/6.3 plan-interaction coverage — the second finish-line gate, under the
  same discipline: I asked whether a tuple can read Covered without the
  interaction actually being exercised, and to not flip if the answer is yes.
  - Differential-fuzzer depth — the last genuinely-open §3 item. The interesting
  part is that Compose is associative but not commutative, so reordering
  children is not relation-preserving; I asked for the honest per-node-kind
  answer including the kinds where no sound restructuring exists.

  Deliberately queued, not forgotten — three items are blocked on
  capability.rs/replace.rs/lower.rs, which the quantifier agent owns: 4.3a/4.3b
  (circumfix C1/C3), 4.4b (MultiTable aliasing), and 4.1 (recursive compounding,
  which the benchmark showed is one of Sena's two refusals). Running them
  concurrently would mean two agents editing the same files, which is how silent
  clobbering happens. They go out as soon as that agent lands.

  Two things that will never close, and shouldn't be counted as gaps:
  MprGroupOverwrite is a permanent carve-out present in two of three reference
  grammars, so "the FST path runs all three with enforcement on" is unreachable
  by design — only via the ADR 0005 override. And SimultaneousRewrite's
  overlapping-subrule configuration stays oracle-blocked until a real hc.dll
  harness exists, which ADR 0001 itself names.

  Still needing you: the upstream real-language-data question, and the submodule
  re-point when PR #465 merges — that one is now build-breaking-relevant, since
  the flipped gate needs those constructs.txt rows to resolve.

● Both finish-line gates are now build-breaking, and the HC-rust port audit's
  gap list is fully resolved.

  Second flip landed (0973f3f). The interesting part was question 3 — could a
  tuple read Covered without the interaction being exercised, i.e. the
  inheritance defect that blocked the sibling flip? The answer is no, for a
  structural reason rather than a lucky count: every AdjacencyTuple is already
  the finest-grained unit (no coarser sibling to borrow from), and coverage is
  credited only from an actual parent-child edge in a per-fixture reified Plan,
  never from co-presence of both node kinds. The one judgment call pushes
  classification toward Uncovered, i.e. unsafe only in the safe direction.
  Proven to bite by suppressing one tuple's credit. It also caught a real doc
  bug: uncovered()'s comment claimed it included ContainsUnsupported rows — had
  that been true, the new assertion would have demanded covering fixtures for
  constructs we deliberately refuse.

  Fuzzer depth closed (5ff068a) — audit §3's last open item. The part worth
  highlighting is the two declines: Compose and Replace got no generator, and
  both are backed by #[should_panic] tests showing build_controllable
  mechanically rejects the reorderings with specific messages. That pins the
  soundness boundary with the real builder rather than a comment — and a
  generator that changed the relation would have made the oracle report false
  disagreements, which is worse than having none. Union did get one.
  Minimisation carries the seed, so a disagreement is replayable.

  One of my own gates caught a live problem, which is the point of it.
  coverage_citation_liveness failed reporting a cited test function that no
  longer exists — the quantifier agent renamed a test cited in the coverage
  ledger. That's exactly the silent-overclaim path the gate was written to
  block, working on its first real encounter. I've told that agent to report the
  old and new names so I can fix the citation myself, and — more importantly —
  that the four other currently-failing tests all assert the old behaviour it's
  deliberately changing, so they must be re-authored with equal rigour, not
  deleted or #[ignore]d. A test that used to prove refusal and now proves
  nothing would be worse than either.

  Audit §3 is now entirely closed or won't-do, and I've made the doc say where
  the real backlog lives instead — tasks.md §4 — so the list can't be mistaken
  for it. Plus the two things that will never close and shouldn't be counted:
  MprGroupOverwrite (permanent carve-out, in two of three reference grammars)
  and SimultaneousRewrite's overlap configuration (oracle-blocked, named by ADR
  0001).

  One agent still running on 4.5/4.6 — the quantifier build, whose acceptance
  test is whether the foma path finally accepts the Indonesian reference grammar
  without --allow-unproven. Three items are queued behind it on file ownership
  (circumfix C1/C3, MultiTable aliasing, recursive compounding).

  I was wrong that the unbounded quantifier was Indonesian's sole blocker. That
  came from reading only the tail of the diagnostic output instead of counting
  every capability-refuse line. Indonesian has three: the quantifier (now
  closed), compounding.non-recursive, and mpr-group.overwrite-output. Verified
  by count.

  The corrected picture is a stronger claim, not a weaker one: MprGroupOverwrite
  is in all three reference grammars, not two. So no reference grammar can ever
  clear the --engine=foma gate without --allow-unproven — not now, not after
  any future work — because that refusal is a structural impossibility for a
  monotone-accumulation admission filter, not an unfinished construction.
  Closing 4.5 and 4.1 shrinks each refusal set but cannot empty any. Benchmark
  doc corrected with the correction marked as such.

  What landed (8fbad70):

  - Unbounded quantifiers compile exactly. The detail I'd flag: the ^>N
  off-by-one is pinned at the compiled-automaton level — render min=2, then
  check the net rejects 1 occurrence and accepts 2 and 3. A string assertion
  would have let a wrong operator through.
  - The RTL mirror bug was reproduced, then fixed — in that order. On t -> d || 
  _ (a b)^{1,N}, the shallow reverse's net requires t followed by "ba" where the
  rule declares "ab". This is exactly why I demanded reproduction first: the
  existing RTL-quantifier fixture wraps a single element, so it's palindromic by
  accident and passed either way. A fix shipped on my structural argument alone
  would have had no failing test behind it.
  - My citation-liveness gate caught a real dangling citation on its first
  encounter — the renamed test left the coverage ledger claiming evidence that
  no longer existed. Fixed and the golden regenerated.
  - I found ~10 doc sites citing an openspec change that didn't exist, so I
  wrote it properly rather than repointing them at
  compile-bounded-fst-quantifiers, whose scope was explicitly bounded-only. It
  records the agent's one-predicate-not-two judgment call, which departs from
  what the decision record proposed. Validate: 28 items.

  - Circumfix C1 — is_structural_rule now checks any allomorph, rule_role's

  five-slot placement, chosen so an off-by-one lands on a different checkable
  pair rather than a symmetric coincidence.

  Two things I want to flag about the recovery itself:

  First, the metathesis work was half-landed in a way that would have looked

  predicate still returned Refuse, making it dead code no grammar could reach. I

  the fixture's presence.

  It reported that confirm-side metathesis looked direction-blind for one
  overlapping case, on evidence it then deleted. I checked, and metathesis.rs
  does thread direction in four places — which makes a blanket claim doubtful.
  It's tracked as an explicitly unverified investigation with both possibilities
  named. I retracted an under-evidenced claim earlier in this session and don't
  intend to repeat it.

  Citation liveness caught its second real dangling reference, and the ledger
  note it fixed had become outright false ("Dir::RightToLeft stays a documented,

● Agent(Resolve SimultaneousRewrite overlap vs the founding oracle) Sonnet 5
  ⎿  Backgrounded agent (↓ to manage · ctrl+o to expand)

● Three agents dispatched. One finding is worth stating now, because it changes
  the strategic picture.

  The "C# oracle harness — zero code exists" record is stale, and I verified 
  that rather than inheriting it. In fact:

  - dotnet 10.0.302 is on PATH
  - HermitCrab builds clean in ~4s; hc.dll builds clean in ~3s
  - A protocol-conforming adapter already exists —
  machine/conformance/adapters/hc-dotnet-wrapper.sh — bridging hc.dll's own CLI
  to the conformance 3-arg contract

  So the harness the project has carried as a large unstarted remainder is
  substantially already built upstream. What was missing was using it. That
  means the one row ADR 0001 names as unverifiable-by-construction —
  SimultaneousRewrite's overlapping subrules, the sole NEEDS-ORACLE verdict in
  the whole table — is reachable now, and an agent is on it.

  I wrote that brief to accept whichever answer is true, and said so explicitly:
  if hc.dll and our engine disagree, that's a real port divergence in exactly
  the case ADR 0001 predicted, and it's the more valuable outcome — report it,
  don't touch the predicate. If hc.dll itself refuses the grammar, that's a
  third answer worth having. This will also be the first fixture in the repo
  whose ground truth comes from the founding oracle rather than from pangloss,
  which the staging plan treats as a strictly stronger artifact.

  Also in flight: 4.4b MultiTable aliasing (with the instruction to demonstrate
  the recall loss before flipping the predicate — the design states it as
  structural, not measured, and closing it means measuring it), and 4.1
  recursive compounding, where I flagged the trap that a containment proof at
  the default max_stem_count of 2 would be vacuously true and must raise the
  confirm cap to mean anything.

  On the hook's other points, to be straight with you: MprGroupOverwrite and the
  WASM compiler removal aren't closeable by more effort. The first is
  structurally unsound for a monotone admission filter — that's a designed
  boundary, and it's why the ADR 0005 override exists. The second is blocked on
  a runtime-payload serializer that needs serde impls across pg_grammar::model
  and pg_featstruct::Interner, which is its own change. The submodule re-point
  needs PR #465 to merge, and the upstream real-language-data question is
  genuinely yours — it's SIL's suite on a shared branch.

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

● All four are in the checklist (#38–41) and planned as Stage 5 in the spine, as
  two openspec changes — 30 items validating. They'll get built alongside the
  rest.

  I split them because visualization stands alone while the other three are a
  chain: timing → thresholds → report.

  A few design calls I made, since they shape what you'll get:

  Visualization gets two properties free from ADR 0002's reified plan — NodeId
  is a content address, so an unchanged grammar renders identically and a diff
  between revisions highlights exactly the subtrees whose meaning changed. I
  specified nodes be labelled by the linguistic work they do (stratum, template,
  rule class), not node kind, since your question is "how is my language
  handled" not "what's the IR". And one rule I made non-negotiable: a node's
  capability verdict must come from the real evaluation, never inferred from the
  node existing — nodes are in the plan whether admitted or not, and a wrong
  label there would lie more persuasively than prose.

  Certification's tiering is the core, and it's where your "some languages may
  never get there" lands. A flat pass/fail would collapse the distinction that
  decides who acts: not-yet (thresholds missed — the language team can add
  lexicon, fix pathological rules) versus not-supported (a refused construct —
  only compiler work moves it, and the report names the predicate). That second
  tier is the "ask for support" signal you described.

  Four honesty rules I wrote as requirements rather than intentions, because a
  certificate is a green light and green lights get cited:
  - A trust=unproven pack (the ADR 0005 override) can never certify — otherwise
  the override becomes the shortest path to a stamp, and the stamp is
  decorative. I flagged it in tasks as the rule whose violation would be most
  convenient.
  - "Held out of training" can't be verified — PanGloss doesn't train, and
  nothing records what an author read. So it's an attestation with an attestor,
  stated unverified. A check that cannot fail isn't a check.
  - ">95% coverage" is specified as a token-level analysis rate, never accuracy
  — a token can get a wrong analysis and still count.
  - Percentiles must name a target device class, or they're unfalsifiable.

  One prediction worth flagging now: under this bar, none of the three reference
  grammars certifies today — all are refused on the compiled path, two by a
  permanent carve-out. I wrote that into the tasks as the expected result, with
  the note that if the report says otherwise, the report is wrong.

  Also: the shell was still inside the machine submodule from the hc.dll build,
  so my mkdir briefly created stray empty dirs there. Removed, submodule
  verified clean — I won't leave junk on a shared branch.

  Three agents still running: 4.4b MultiTable, 4.1 recursive compounding, and
  the hc.dll oracle comparison for SimultaneousRewrite.

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

● The oracle agent hit an API error mid-task, but it had already done the
  irreplaceable part — running hc.dll — so I finished it myself rather than
  restarting.

  The one row ADR 0001 says we cannot self-verify is now verified, and the two 
  engines agree.

  hc.dll loaded a genuinely-overlapping simultaneous-subrule grammar, compiled
  it without error, and its (word, signature) output is byte-identical to 
  pg_parse::Morpher's across all 9 words — diffed mechanically.

  The agreement discriminates, which is what makes it evidence. I want to be
  precise here, because a weaker reading was available: both engines return no 
  analysis for the underlying form pe, and agreement on a shared silence would
  be equally consistent with both engines simply failing on overlap. The
  load-bearing result is the pair:

  - be analyzes — overlap resolved by subrule 1 (declared first)
  - de does not — the same position resolved by subrule 2

  Had subrule 2 won, those two results would be exactly swapped. So what's
  verified is the resolution order, not mutual silence.

  The "harness doesn't exist" blocker was stale, and that's the bigger finding.
  The project had carried the C# oracle harness as a large unstarted remainder.
  In fact hc.dll builds in ~3s and
  machine/conformance/adapters/hc-dotnet-wrapper.sh already bridges it to the
  conformance protocol. What was missing was using it — so task 5.2's premise
  that this needed add-reference-hermitcrab-parity §§2-5 built first was simply
  wrong.

  What I did not let this license, and said so in both the fixture and row 6:
  the proposer-side refusal is unaffected and still correct.
  simultaneous.subrule-overlap continues to refuse on the compiled path with a
  real span-intersection witness — that's a construction question (can the
  proposer represent overlap?), entirely separate from oracle trust. Row 6
  becomes an ordinary open construct row, not a closed one. And the verification
  doesn't generalize past two subrules sharing one focus with one shared class
  member; three-way overlap, left-environment overlap, and overlap with a
  feature-changing output each need their own oracle run.

  This is also the repo's first fixture with founding-oracle ground truth rather
  than pangloss — which the staging plan treats as strictly stronger, and which
  means upstream acceptance wouldn't need to re-derive it. I kept output.tsv
  and hc_stderr.txt verbatim as raw provenance, and deleted the throwaway dump
  test the interrupted agent left behind.

  Two agents still running: 4.4b MultiTable aliasing and 4.1 recursive
  compounding.

● Agent(Extend the compound loop to multi-level) Sonnet 5
  ⎿  Backgrounded agent (↓ to manage · ctrl+o to expand)

● 4.1's depth bound is closed, and the agent made the right call not to promote 
  past it.

  The finding is precise: emit.rs's "bounded compound loop" hardcodes exactly
  one extra root regardless of any rule's max_apps. Since every recursive
  compounding configuration needs ≥3 stems, that path is structurally incapable
  of proposing one — verified against the real FomaProposer, which offers zero
  candidates for a three-stem word while still handling depth-1 compounds on the
  same net. So promoting the predicate would have claimed a capability that
  doesn't exist.

  Two things I checked rather than accepted:

  - "Always finite" holds. CompoundingRuleDef::max_apps is u16, so no -1
  sentinel is even representable — unlike QuantifierPattern's genuine Kleene
  case. That means design.md row 2's "stays refused if unboundable" escape valve
  never applies to Compounding, and I've told the follow-on not to reintroduce
  it.
  - The containment gap is non-vacuous, which was the trap I'd flagged in the
  brief. At the default max_stem_count = 2 confirm also returns zero, so a naive
  containment test would have compared 0 ⊆ 0 and proved nothing while looking
  like evidence. Raised to 3, confirm genuinely finds an analysis — so propose ⊉
  confirm is real and checked.

  The agent also caught a double-counting bug in its own first draft of the
  closure (a cycle re-discovering the origin rule as its own ancestor) and
  pinned the fix with an equivalence test against the existing boolean
  classifier. And it was straight about one limit: it didn't do a destructive
  git-revert to get an independent "before" build, so its "Sena refusal count
  unchanged" rests on the verdict branch being byte-identical to what it read.

  I've dispatched the remaining half — extending the compound loop to multiple
  levels, consuming the depth bound as the single source of truth. Cost is the
  real risk there, so the brief requires measuring the growth and failing on the
  budget with a typed outcome rather than hanging: refusing because a depth is
  unaffordable is honest and is a different answer from refusing because the
  construction is missing.

  4.1's work can't be committed yet — it shares capability.rs with the
  still-running MultiTable agent, and the new test asserts diagnostic text from
  that file, so splitting them would break HEAD.

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

● 4.4b is done, and the design's one open question is now settled by measurement
  rather than argument.

  The design deliberately stated the recall loss as structural, not measured —
  so closing it meant measuring it. The agent did exactly that, in the right
  order: pre_fix_equivalent_rule_never_fires_on_table_a_originated_material
  builds a pre-fix-equivalent net and shows the rule silently fails to fire on
  the other table's material; its sibling shows the aliased path now rewrites
  it. The false-negative direction the design argued for was correct, and
  reproduced before being closed.

  Worth noting the design's own cost fear didn't materialize: threading TableId
  cost one changed call site, not the ~40 feared, because only
  compile_rewrite_rule_subset needed the alias-aware alphabet and
  SegAlphabet::new stayed untouched. No public signature changed.

  Two real findings I've recorded rather than let slide:

  1. The aliasing doesn't reach metathesis. compile_metathesis_swap_net renders
  tokens directly rather than through render_slots, so a metathesis rule in a
  shared-representation grammar is still exposed to exactly the false negative
  that was just fixed everywhere else. The agent flagged this itself rather than
  quietly leaving it. Exposure is advisory-only today, but the honest boundary
  moved without that path covered — and it widened slightly when RTL metathesis
  began compiling more shapes.
  2. Morpher::signature() renders an empty surface half on a cross-stratum word,
  even though the morpheme-level analysis is correct. Confirmed by experiment
  to be not multi-table-specific, so it's a general defect. The hypothesis —
  synthesis never updating a candidate's .stratum the way analysis does — is
  recorded as a hypothesis. This one matters more than it looks: signature is
  what the batch TSV and every parity diff compare on.

  I also intervened on a bad fix before it happened. Seven pg-cli tests started
  failing because they use a recursive-compounding grammar as their stand-in for
  "a refused grammar" — reasonable until the in-flight agent began promoting
  that construct. I've told it explicitly not to weaken the tests, revert the
  promotion, or keep something artificially refused, and to repoint them at
  mpr-group.overwrite-output instead: a permanent carve-out is a stable refusal
  fixture, whereas any ConfigPredicate construct is a moving target that breaks
  again on the next promotion. Several of those tests pin the ADR 0005
  indelible-trust=unproven stamp — which is precisely what stops the override
  becoming a back door to a certification pass under the new Stage 5 plan — so
  they must keep their strength.

  Nothing is committed yet: capability.rs carries three agents' interleaved
  work, and the compounding agent is mid-promotion.

  The peer agent made the right call, and I checked rather than took its word:
  all 39 pg-cli tests pass, the ADR 0005 indelible-trust=unproven assertions are
  intact, and it added a doc comment enumerating exactly which constructs must

  Reduplication, MultiTable, QuantifierPattern, SimultaneousRewrite — because

  What I fixed on review: the swap left the names pointing at the old subject.
  Three local bindings were still compounding, four test functions were still
  named ..._refuses_compounding_... / ..._proceeds_for_compounding, and a
  temp-dir label still said no-flags-compounding — all while exercising an
  MprGroupOverwrite grammar. A test named for a construct it no longer touches
  is a small lie that costs someone an hour later. I confirmed none of those
  names were cited in coverage_ledger.rs before renaming (the citation-liveness
  gate would have caught it, but checking first is cheaper), and re-ran it
  after: 3/3 green.

  Workspace verification is running now.

  One thing I want to flag about what this episode revealed, since it's a
  pattern rather than an incident: the promotion of a construct silently 
  invalidated tests elsewhere that depended on it being refused. That coupling
  wasn't visible from either agent's file ownership — the compounding agent had
  no reason to look in pg-cli, and pg-cli had no reason to expect its fixture's
  disposition to move. The doc comment addresses the specific case; the general
  lesson is that "which construct is currently refused" is an implicit contract
  that spans crates, and the only stable anchors are the permanent carve-outs.

  Still in flight: the compounding agent's multi-level compound loop, which is
  the promotion those tests were reacting to. Nothing committed yet —
  capability.rs still carries three agents' interleaved work.

  Every reference grammar is now down to exactly one refusal — and in all three 
  it's the permanent carve-out. Re-counting every capability-refuse line against
  a freshly built release binary:

  ┌────────────┬────────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────┐
  │  Grammar   │              Was               │            Now             │
  ├────────────┼────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤

  │            │ MPR-overwrite                  │                            │
  ├────────────┼────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ Amharic    │ MPR-overwrite                  │ mpr-group.overwrite-output │
  ├────────────┼────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ Sena       │ compounding + MPR-overwrite    │ mpr-group.overwrite-output │
  └────────────┴────────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────┘

  quantifier.bounded-expansion and compounding.non-recursive are gone from all
  three. The benchmark doc predicted exactly this — closing 4.5 and 4.1 "reduces
  each grammar's refusal set but cannot empty any of them" — so the prediction
  held, and what remains is the irreducible part rather than a mix of reducible
  and irreducible. I counted every line this time rather than reading the tail.

  Both builds followed the demonstrate-then-fix order, which is what makes them
  trustworthy: recursive compounding's gap was shown empirically (zero
  candidates for a three-stem word while depth-1 worked on the same net) before
  the loop was extended; MultiTable's recall loss was reproduced with a
  pre-fix-equivalent net before the aliasing closed it. That was the one thing
  the MultiTable design left deliberately open — stated as structural, not
  measured — and it's now measured.

  I checked two claims rather than accepting them: max_apps is u16, so no
  unbounded sentinel is representable and compounding genuinely has no
  unboundable shape; and containment was made non-vacuous — at the default
  max_stem_count = 2 confirm also returns zero, so a naive test would have
  compared 0 ⊆ 0 and proved nothing while looking like evidence.

  Two residual findings recorded rather than buried: the aliasing doesn't reach
  compile_metathesis_swap_net, which renders tokens directly, so metathesis in a
  shared-representation grammar keeps the same false-negative exposure; and
  Morpher::signature() renders an empty surface half on a cross-stratum word —
  confirmed not multi-table-specific, so a general defect, and it matters
  because signature is what every parity diff compares on.

● The compounding agent's final report is in, and my commit already captured its
  work. Three follow-ups from it, handled:

  1. f3_parity's Amharic leg — it honestly reported "started, did not observe
  completion" rather than assuming. I'm running it to completion now; still
  going (~16 min).

  2. A real residual gap I've recorded (#44): emit_underlying_templated — the P6
  templated path — still has the one-level compound loop. The agent correctly
  left it alone as out of scope, but it matters more than "experimental"
  suggests: per the FST-only decision record, the P6 templated emitter is what
  made Aweti-shaped grammars emit at all. So a templated grammar that also
  compounds recursively would silently under-propose on that path while the
  default path handles it — and since the predicate is now ConfirmOnly
  unconditionally, nothing gates that. Either extend it (it can reuse
  build_compound_chain) or make the templated route refuse with a named witness.
  Not left silently under-proposing.

  3. One thing it flagged that didn't need fixing. It reported a "cosmetic stale
  citation" in cover_mpr_groups.rs. I checked: the cited test
  head_a_word_over_propose_confirm_prune still exists, so the citation is live.
  Nothing to fix — worth verifying rather than dutifully "fixing" a non-problem.

  Two details from its report worth surfacing, because they're the kind of thing
  that's easy to get wrong:

  - Growth is linear, not exponential. One extra {Pfx,Roots,Next} triple per
  level. The apparent exponential blowup is a property of the compiled
  automaton's accepted language, not of emitted text size — a distinction that
  would have justified refusing this work if gotten backwards.
  - It added a separate budget dimension (DEFAULT_COMPOUND_CHAIN_DEPTH_BUDGET = 
  200) rather than reusing chain_depth_cap, which governs a different per-word
  apply-time recursion. That's the "don't conflate dimensions" discipline the
  codebase already established, and it means an oversized grammar refuses on
  cost with a typed outcome — distinct from refusing on capability. Verified
  with a multipleApplication="60000" grammar: typed refusal before any emission,
  no hang.

  I also updated the typology-basis doc to close out its gap list with outcomes
  rather than deleting it — including the two places its own predictions were
  wrong (MultiTable's fix was smaller than feared; the unbounded quantifier
  mattered more, since it was blocking a reference grammar rather than just a
  coverage row).

  Remaining: 4.2's further RTL shapes, 4.3c (coupled to Reduplication), the P6
  templated loop, the two findings from 4.4b, and the Stage 5 work.

  you asked for, three agents on file-disjoint pieces:

  Visualization (#38) — plan JSON → mermaid. I emphasized two things from the
  design that are easy to get wrong. First, the capability verdict per node must
  come from the real evaluation, never inferred from a node's presence: a node
  exists in the plan whether or not it was admitted, so an inferred label would
  make the diagram assert a construct is handled when it's refused — and a
  picture lies more persuasively than prose. Second, the content-address
  property that makes revision-diffing work has to be pinned as a test, not
  assumed: change one rule, and that node's and its ancestors' identities must
  move while unrelated subtrees' don't. All three reference grammars are now a
  good live test of the refusal marking, since each refuses on exactly one
  predicate.

  Timing harness (#39) — the HC-rust vs foma speedup table. I steered it toward
  driving the engines in-process rather than parsing the batch TSV, which
  sidesteps the integer-millisecond floor entirely rather than working around
  it. And I told it the refusal case is the common case on the foma path, not an
  edge case, so it must be designed for first — a refused fixture needs its own
  outcome row naming the predicate, never a zero and never a dropped row. I
  also asked it to be honest about timing noise: if a number is too noisy to be
  meaningful at these fixture sizes, saying so beats a confident wrong figure.

  The metathesis cross-table gap (#42) — the one 4.4b flagged and left. Worth
  doing now because the honest boundary already moved (that predicate is
  ConfirmOnly now, so nothing gates the path) and the exposure widened when RTL
  metathesis landed. I flagged a specific hazard: metathesis must reproduce the
  same value at its swapped output position, so if an atom becomes a union of
  aliased tokens, the construction must not let the input match one alias and
  the output emit a different one — that would be a worse bug than the one being
  fixed. If aliasing genuinely can't compose with the cross-product shape,
  naming it as a refused shape is an acceptable outcome; a named refusal beats a
  silent false negative.

  Certification and make-report (#40, #41) come after the timing harness, since
  they consume it.

● I investigated the reported "signature bug" myself while the agents work, and
  it isn't one. Both halves of the report were wrong, and I checked rather than
  queuing it as a defect:

  The Rust behaviour is faithful. AnalysisStratumRule.cs:109 is the only site in
  the entire C# HermitCrab that assigns a Word's stratum during parsing — and
  C# synthesis never reassigns it either. That matches this port exactly: our
  sole assignment is in analyze() at stratum.rs:1242, and synthesis only ever
  reads input.stratum as a depth guard. So "synthesis doesn't set it" is the
  correct behaviour, not the defect.

  The empty surface half has a different, already-known cause. C# renders a
  surface via Stratum.CharacterDefinitionTable, so a word whose shape carries
  inner-stratum char-defs while its stratum is the outer one has no
  representations to render. That's the limitation the audit already records —
  and whose note reads, verbatim, "recorded so it is not re-diagnosed as a 
  MultiTable bug."

  It got re-diagnosed anyway. The note did its job; what failed was reading it.
  So I've written the disposal out in full at that same location rather than
  leaving it implicit, including the practical consequence that's easy to trip
  over: a cross-stratum fixture must compare at the structured morpheme-id
  level, not on signature. That's what the existing two-table test established
  and what the new one follows.

  I also separated the two questions that were tangled together: no code change
  is warranted here, and a signature-level workaround would be treating a
  symptom — but lifting the limitation so inner-stratum roots actually render is
  real, larger work in surface tokenization. That should be opened as that, not
  as a signature bug.

  My independent f3_parity also confirms 4.1: 3 passed, 914s (the agent measured
  899s).

  Three agents still running — visualization, timing harness, and the metathesis
  cross-table gap.

  header, separator and every data row of every table in the real document,

● All three landed and committed. Workspace 1300 passed, 0 failed, five gates

  The most important thing to come out of this is a defect in the oracle itself,
  and it's worth stating carefully because it changes how some earlier claims
  should be read.

  The confirm engine — the reference everything else is measured against — is
  table-blind for phonological rules. pg-rules/src/cache.rs:49 declares const 
  TABLE: TableId = TableId(0) and feeds it to rewrite's cache, metathesis's
  cache, and environment-allomorph compilation; metathesis.rs:497 and :646
  hardcode the same. That is precisely the implicit-char_tables[0] mistake class
  owning_table was introduced to remove on the compiled side, and that 4.4b
  just closed for rendering — the oracle side never got the equivalent fix.

  Two consequences:

  - pg_parse::Morpher cannot analyze a cross-table metathesis word at all, which
  is why the new fixture honestly pins that word expect_fail and demonstrates
  recall against the compiled net directly rather than against a broken
  reference.
  - More significantly: containment proofs for multi-table grammars are 
  currently measured against a wrong reference. MultiTable's ConfirmOnly
  disposition rests on an oracle that is itself table-blind. That doesn't
  invalidate the aliasing fix — the reproduce-then-fix evidence stands on the
  compiled net — but it does mean the multi-table oracle comparisons are weaker
  than they look. Recorded as its own change, since it's pg-rules.

  It also explains something the agent couldn't account for: it tested making
  one TableId(0) site coincidentally correct and that didn't fix the analysis.
  Of course not — three other sites share the constant.

  On the metathesis fix itself, the agent got the hazard right. I'd flagged that
  a swap must echo the same matched value at its transposed position. Rather
  than take the obvious route through render_slots' text-level union, it
  verified that would be unsafe and instead aliased the candidate set, leaving
  the per-branch cross-product intact — so each branch fixes one concrete
  char-def and the swap permutes that literal assignment. Pinned exhaustively:
  every aliased/non-aliased combination yields output within {unchanged, correct
  swap}, never a third value.

  And the speedup data is more useful than "it's faster": 2.31× on one typology,
  but 0.61× and 0.40× on two others, plus 4 of 8 language fixtures refused
  outright. The per-typology grouping you asked for is exactly what makes the
  losses visible — an aggregate would have buried them.

  Remaining from Stage 5: certification (#40) and make-report (#41), which
  consume the harness. Also open: Aweti recall at 68/106, the P6 templated
  compound loop, the pre-existing FomaAnalyzer::new multi-table panic, and this
  new oracle defect.

  One result worth reporting because it's a negative: I re-ran the Aweti recall
  after the P6 templated compound-loop fix, and it's still 68/106. So that fix
  didn't move Aweti coverage. That's not surprising — Aweti's known blockers are
  the apply_up explosion and missing truncation-drop semantics, not compounding
  — but I wanted to check rather than let "we fixed the templated path" imply
  progress on recall it didn't produce.

  The emit.rs pair is done, and the root cause was worth the hunt. collect_roots
  took a single table for the whole grammar — always the last stratum's — and
  indexed every root allomorph's char-defs against it. On the two-stratum
  fixture, the inner stratum's table has 4 segments and the outer has 3, so an
  inner root's char-def id 3 was looked up against a 3-entry table. That's the
  len is 3 / index is 3 message exactly. The fix removes the table parameter
  entirely and resolves per-stratum — eliminating the ability to pass the wrong
  table rather than passing the right one, which is the better shape.

  I proved the sweep isn't vacuous, since the agent honestly said it hadn't. I
  reintroduced the bug at that one line and the all-fixtures sweep failed with
  PANICKED on 1 fixture(s); restored, green. That sweep now drives all 35
  discovered fixtures through FomaAnalyzer::new, so a crash can't hide behind a
  fixture whose own gate takes a different path — which is precisely how this
  one hid.

  I also fixed a clippy warning the extraction introduced rather than leaving
  it: the new shared budget-check returns a 192-byte Err. I allowed the lint
  with a written justification — the Err payload is the emitter's complete
  honest outcome carrying its own diagnostics, and boxing it would add
  indirection purely to satisfy a size heuristic while obscuring the "return it
  verbatim" contract. Remaining emit.rs warnings are all pre-existing.

  Certification landed with the shape I most wanted: Threshold<T>{ value, 
  calibration } where Calibration is Measured{citation} or
  Placeholder{rationale} — a first-class field, not a comment, so no number can
  quietly pass as evidence. Device class is dev-workstation-v1, deliberately not
  a mobile name since no mobile device has been benchmarked. The override
  sabotage test is properly constructed: it asserts the report certifies under
  Proven, then flips only the trust field and shows every check become Blocked
  and the tier NotSupported. And all three reference grammars come out
  not_supported on mpr-group.overwrite-output — the predicted result.

  Holding the commit: the oracle agent is still mid-edit in pg-rules, and
  pg-foma depends on it, so splitting now risks a HEAD that doesn't compile.

  Meanwhile, the oracle work found considerably more than I briefed it for.

  The escalation that matters: the defect was also live in rewrite.rs's 
  production hot paths — synthesize_with_mpr_cached and analyze_cached, the
  entry points for every ordinary rewrite rule reached through crate::stratum.
  Those independently re-derive table_id for output construction even though the
  matcher was already correctly cached. So any rewrite rule on a non-zero-table
  stratum had its output built against table 0. I named five sites; it fixed
  eleven.

  And the single most valuable finding is a suite blind spot, not a code bug.
  Every existing multi-table fixture uses FeatureNaturalClass — and the Feature
  branch of nat_class_lanes never reads self.table, because feature lanes are
  table-agnostic by construction. So this entire bug class was structurally 
  invisible to the conformance suite, because the suite routed around
  cross-table ambiguity via features. That's why it survived: nothing could see
  it. The new unit test is the first thing in the repo to exercise the
  table-dependent path against a non-zero table, verified by reverting the fix
  and watching it panic.

  Two instances remain, both located precisely and left out of scope
  deliberately: morph.rs:97's constant (reachable from production, affects
  allomorph LHS/RHS patterns), and the cross-table word's real blocker —
  is_match_traced rendering via g.strata[n-1].table unconditionally, exposed
  only because synthesis_reorder relocates a segment without resetting its
  char_def the way every rewrite path does. Both recorded with citations; the
  fixture's expect_fail correctly stands rather than being flipped.

  One process-hygiene note I'll carry forward: agents killing processes by name
  in a shared checkout can take out the coordinator's runs. That's my own
  instruction to fix — I should tell them to never kill by image name.

  entire point of the owning_table contract is that table zero is never an
  implicit default, and a boolean preserves that default in one arm while making
  correctness a special case.

  I've asked for the owning TableId (or Option<TableId>) instead, so one path is
  correct for one table and N tables alike — but I explicitly invited pushback:
  if the behavioural difference is genuinely binary (whether to reset char_def
  rather than which table to resolve against), then a boolean is right and I'd
  rather have the reasoning than the change.

  let still_valid = n.char_def != NO_CHAR_DEF
      && (n.char_def as usize) < table.len()
      && flat_unifiable(&n.lanes,
  table.get(CharDefId(n.char_def)).feature_lanes());

  Using flat_unifiable — the same predicate matching_reps_for_node's own
  fallback uses — makes the test "will the eventual surface-match gate actually
  accept this identity", not a proxy for it. That gives one code path that is
  correct for one table and N tables alike:

  - single-table: the check always passes, so nothing is cleared — and crucially
  "table 0 is fine" becomes a verified fact rather than an assumption
  - genuinely cross-table: out of bounds or non-unifying, correctly cleared
  - real alias where the raw index happens to denote the right entry: kept, so
  no information is discarded for no reason

  That third case is the one a boolean would have gotten wrong, and it's why the
  objection was worth raising.

❯ Those 3 issues - I don't understand MprGroupOverwrite and monotone admission

● Fair — that one's genuinely opaque without the context. Let me get it exactly

  Searched for 2 patterns (ctrl+o to expand)

● Good — and I'll record the calibration deferral as your decision. Here's
  MprGroupOverwrite, from the ground up.

  What MPR features are. Little tags a rule sticks on a word to control which
  later rules may apply. A rule can say "only apply if the word carries tag X"
  (requiredMPRFeatures) or "never apply if it carries X" (excludedMPRFeatures).
  Groups are named sets of these tags with a policy.

  The two policies differ in one crucial way (MprFeatureSet.AddOutput,
  MprFeatureSet.cs:29-44):

  - Append — adding a tag is pure union. The set only ever grows.
  - Overwrite — adding a tag from group G first drops every other member of G,
  then adds. So a tag that was present can disappear.

  "Monotone admission filter" is what the compiled FST is. The proposer models
  MPR state as accumulation — only-grows — because that's what a compiled net
  can do without remembering how it got to a word. For Append that's exact. For
  Overwrite it's wrong, and wrong in the fatal direction:

  1. Monotone accumulation computes a superset of the true tag set (it never
  drops anything).
  2. But mpr_excluded_ok blocks a rule when the word has a listed tag
  (model.rs:913-920).
  3. So a superset of tags means more rules wrongly blocked → valid analyses
  never proposed.

  That's recall loss — the one error propose-and-confirm cannot repair. Confirm
  prunes over-proposals; it can't invent an analysis the proposer never offered.
  Over-approximating in one place under-approximates in another.

  Why not just track the history? Because whether tag X is present depends on
  which rule overwrote group G and in what order — that's derivational history,
  and a compiled FST is a relation over strings with no memory of how it got
  there. And you can't fork on "both possibilities" either: the fork isn't
  recoverable from the string, so it'd have to branch on unobservable state at
  every Overwrite group.

  One practical caveat worth knowing: in every reference grammar today the
  groups are singletons, so Overwrite-vs-Append is unobservable there. This is a
  latent correctness boundary, not something currently mis-parsing your data.
  It's refused because it could be, not because it is.

  And there is an on-ramp. The ADR 0005 override force-compiles it — you ship,
  but the artifact carries an indelible trust=unproven stamp and certification
  refuses it. That's the honest trade rather than a hard wall.

  Read 1 file, ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Using the wait to record the Aweti outcome in its plan doc — it's the plan of
  record for Tasks 4–9 and has never been committed.

  Read 1 file, ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

❯ So, is the plan "make too many candidates and then filter with HC?"           

● Yes — that's exactly it, and it's called propose-and-confirm here. But two
  qualifications matter, because "too many then filter" undersells the
  constraint that makes it work.

  The asymmetry is the whole design. The FST proposer may over-generate freely,
  but it must never omit a valid analysis. Over-proposal costs time and confirm
  throws the extras away. Omission is unrecoverable — confirm can only prune a
  candidate set, it can't invent an analysis that was never offered. That's why
  nearly every correctness decision this session came down to "which direction
  does this error go": the shallow reversed_slots reverse, the cross-table token
  mismatch, the monotone MPR filter — all were dangerous specifically because
  they caused omission, not over-proposal.

  And "too many" has to stay bounded, or you've just moved the cost. If the
  proposer emits a sloppy superset, confirm becomes the bottleneck again and
  you've gained nothing. That's why the compiler refuses constructs it can't
  represent faithfully rather than approximating them — an unfaithful proposer
  either omits (fatal) or floods confirm (pointless). It's also why the dead-end
  census exists: when confirm is slow, it's because the superset is full of
  candidates that die late.

  The measured reality from this session is that it's a win 
  construct-by-construct, not universally:

  ┌──────────────────────────────────┬─────────────┬─────────────────┬───────┐
  │             typology             │  complete   │ propose+confirm │       │
  │                                  │   engine    │                 │       │
  ├──────────────────────────────────┼─────────────┼─────────────────┼───────┤
  │ suffixing-evidential-adjacency   │ 0.594 ms    │ 0.257 ms        │ 2.31× │
  ├──────────────────────────────────┼─────────────┼─────────────────┼───────┤
  │ polysynthetic-stratal-derivation │ 0.233 ms    │ 0.380 ms        │ 0.61× │
  ├──────────────────────────────────┼─────────────┼─────────────────┼───────┤
  │ truncate-morphotactic            │ 0.066 ms    │ 0.167 ms        │ 0.40× │
  └──────────────────────────────────┴─────────────┴─────────────────┴───────┘

  So on some shapes the proposal overhead exceeds what it saves. Where it really
  pays is the tail: the full engine's Amharic p99 is 105 seconds with a worst
  word at 7.6 minutes — that's the exhaustive search the FST is meant to
  short-circuit, and it's the reason the architecture exists at all.

  One more piece: confirm isn't a dumb filter — it's the same complete
  HermitCrab engine, run on a handful of candidates instead of searching the
  whole space. That's why the oracle table-blindness bug I fixed earlier
  mattered so much: if the filter is wrong, every containment proof measured
  against it is measuring against a wrong reference.

  Parity's still running; two agents on the last §4 items. I'll commit Aweti as
  soon as it lands.

● Aweti is committed (f892cfd) with f3_parity 3/3 confirming it. On your
  question — your instinct is right, and it lands on two real things, one of
  which is already sitting in the vendored dependency.

  Your "X and not-X" is a dual-rail encoding, and the established mathematics is
  Belnap's four-valued logic / bilattices (Ginsberg, Fitting). Keep two sets —
  asserted and denied — and make accumulation componentwise union. Then
  Overwrite of group G by output O becomes:

  asserted ∪= O
  denied   ∪= (G \ O)

  Both only ever grow, so the encoding is monotone — even though the derived
  truth value isn't. That's precisely the trick: you get four states (unknown,
  true, false, contradiction) out of a monotone substrate.

  The crux is that fourth state. X ∈ asserted ∧ X ∈ denied means the word passed
  through conflicting overwrites — and the encoding can't distinguish "set then
  cleared" from "cleared then set". That's where order-dependence resurfaces.
  But here's why it might still work: under propose-and-confirm we don't need
  exactness, only no omission. If contradiction is treated as "possibly present,
  possibly absent — admit both", that's over-approximation in the safe
  direction, and confirm prunes. If that holds, the carve-out falls.

  And the practical primitive already exists. I checked the vendored foma crate:
  it implements flag diacritics — flag_diacritics_end_to_end at apply.rs:2457,
  suppression at :1374, consistency checking at :1859. XFST/foma flags are the
  standard mechanism for exactly this: @P. set, @N. negative-set, @R. require,
  @D. disallow, and @C.f@ clear — which is Overwrite, with @D. matching
  excludedMPRFeatures.

  My one real worry, and I've told the agent it's the likely killer: flag
  diacritics are an apply-time mechanism, and this project composes everything
  into one net and then uses fsm_intersect plus upper projection. We have direct
  evidence those diverge — the bug I just fixed was symbols invisible to
  apply_up being fatal to fsm_intersect. So flags surviving
  compose/minimize/intersect is the thing to test, not assume.

  I've sent a research brief covering both leads, requiring a throwaway probe
  against the actual vendored source rather than general knowledge, and asked
  for a verdict with the obstruction named either way — because a
  proven-impossible result would let us document the carve-out as proven rather
  than merely believed. I also told it to be skeptical of its own enthusiasm: a
  wrong "yes" would give us a proposer that silently omits analyses in a
  construct we currently refuse honestly, which is strictly worse than today.

  Two other agents still running on the last §4 items (RTL shapes, circumfix
  C2).

● You're right, and it changed the task materially — I'd framed it as "find the
  encoding," which is the wrong shape for an AND-OR plan model.

  The reframe I sent: enumerate candidate constructions, characterise which
  grammar shapes each admits soundly, and register them as alternatives. That's
  already how Compounding, RightToLeftRewrite, MultiTable and QuantifierPattern
  work — all ConfigPredicate with per-configuration verdicts. A construction
  admitting only some shapes is a success, not a partial answer.

  One measurement while framing it, and it cuts both ways. From mpr_add_output's
  code, to_remove = group.members & !output. For a singleton group, if it's
  touched then its only member is in the output, so nothing is ever dropped —
  Overwrite is exactly Append, provably. That's a free, exactly-sound
  construction.

  But the doc's claim that reference grammars use singleton groups is wrong as a
  universal:

  indonesian:  1-member + 3-member  (mpr2 mpr3 mpr4)
  amharic:     1-member + 5-member  (mpr2..mpr6)
  sena:        3-member             (mpr1 mpr2 mpr3)

  And Overwrite is the default when the attribute is absent (load.rs:368-369).
  So the singleton predicate admits none of the three. Worth registering anyway;
  not the win.

  The lead I think actually unblocks them is graph-theoretic, which is your 
  point exactly. A drop only happens when a rule's output touches an Overwrite
  group while the word already holds a different member of that group. So: can 
  any reachable derivation put two distinct members of one group on the same 
  word? If not, no drop is reachable, monotone accumulation is exact, and the
  grammar is admissible.

  That's a reachability analysis over "which rules add which members, and can
  they co-occur" — and it's the same idiom this codebase already used twice:
  compounding_max_depth's transitive closure over the feeds-edge, and
  compounding_recursive's rule-graph reachability. I pointed the agent at both
  as precedent, since "turn a boolean into a bound" is precisely the move that
  worked there.

  So four constructions now, each with its admitting shape: singleton (exact,
  free), drop-unreachable (the promising one), dual-rail/bilattice with
  contradiction-as-admit-both (general fallback), and flag diacritics (still
  worth testing for fsm_intersect survival).

  Meanwhile Aweti is committed at 100/106 with f3_parity 3/3, and two agents are
  still closing the last §4 items.

● Research is out. Now the grill — and I want to start by naming the thing I
  think is the real risk in this design, because it's the one that decides
  everything else.

  The skill needs a validation case, and you already have two

  A process document that mandates "assess at least 3 multi-tree models" will
  produce two strawmen and a favourite unless something forces the candidates to
  come from measurement. The dead-end-census skill works precisely because it
  makes you gather d1–d6 attribution first, so the candidate encodings are
  generated by the data rather than brainstormed.

  So my proposed acceptance test for this skill: run it retroactively against 
  two cases whose answers we already know.

  - MprGroupOverwrite (trigger e) — the live one. Would the process have led to
  "singleton groups are exactly Append" and "drop-unreachability is a
  graph-reachability predicate"? Or would it have led to the dual-rail bilattice
  encoding, which is general, elegant, and the wrong first move?
  - Aweti (triggers a/b/d) — the P6 templated emitter. Would the process have
  found "templated emission" or would it have proposed three variants of
  enumeration, which was the thing that couldn't work?

  If the skill can't reproduce those two, it's ceremony. This also gives us
  something to write down that isn't invented.

  Four places I think your brainstorm is underspecified

  1. Trigger (e) is a different animal from (a)–(d), and conflating them is 
  dangerous. (a)–(d) are measurable properties of a working compile. (e) is a
  capability refusal, which already has a whole pipeline — ADR 0001
  dispositions, capability.rs predicates, an openspec change. The risk is the
  skill telling someone to go re-architect when the correct terminal state is
  "we refuse this honestly and that's right." I'd keep (e) in — the MPR work is
  exactly this shape and it is a graph-transform problem — but the skill must
  force an explicit answer to "is refusing correct here?" before any model is
  considered.

  2. A performance conformance fixture is not the same artifact as a correctness
  one, and the obvious version will be flaky. Correctness fixtures are exact:
  words → expected analyses. Pinning wall-clock milliseconds pins the machine.
  My strong recommendation: pin structural, deterministic proxies — states/arcs,
  artifact bytes, and candidates-proposed-per-confirmed-analysis. Those are
  exactly reproducible, they're the quantities that actually cause the symptom,
  and they can be build-breaking without being flaky. Wall-clock goes in the
  benchmark matrix as a tracked number, not a gate.

  3. "Don't degrade other grammars" needs teeth, and this repo has hard evidence
  about what happens without them. A new fixture pins the new grammar; it says
  nothing about whether you loosened a different one. The
  coverage-gate-inheritance trap went build-breaking two days ago for exactly
  this reason — a coarse gate let coverage be silently inherited. Same failure
  is available here. The honest cost is a full f3_parity run (~16 min) plus the
  benchmark matrix per change, and I think the skill should just say so rather
  than pretend there's a cheap version.

  4. Who is holding this skill? If it's for grammar authors, it cannot require
  five ADRs and a 16-minute gate. If it's for engineers here, it can. Your
  brainstorm reads engineer-facing — which I think is right, because the
  linguist-facing on-ramp is the thing we just built: make-report/certification
  flags the language, then an engineer runs this. Worth stating that hand-off
  explicitly so the skill isn't written for two audiences at once.

  One thing I want to add

  The skill needs a legitimate negative-result exit: "three models assessed,
  none beats current, record and stop." You made this point yourself about
  certification — some languages may never get there, and that's the point.
  Without that exit the skill applies pressure to ship a change, which is how
  we'd get a clever transform that helps one grammar and quietly loosens the
  proposer everywhere else. dead-end-census already has this shape: E1/E3/E4 are
  parked, build-ready, not dead — verdicts are per-corpus, not permanent.

  Two questions before I draft anything:

  Is my read on the fixture question right — structural counts as the gate,
  wall-clock as a tracked number only? That's the biggest single design call
  here and it determines whether the fixtures are trustworthy or noise.

  And on the "broadly useful" weighting you asked for — do you want that as a
  genuine tiebreaker (a scoring term that can lose to a big single-grammar win),
  or as a veto (a change that helps only one grammar shape doesn't ship at
  all)? Those produce very different skills, and I don't want to guess.

  All six groups are Overwrite (no output= attribute anywhere → load.rs:369's

  ┌────────────┬─────────────────┬──────────────────────────┐
  │  Grammar   │      Group      │       Writes to it       │
  ├────────────┼─────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ indonesian │ mpr1 (1 member) │ one — MPRFeatures="mpr1" │
  ├────────────┼─────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ indonesian │ mpr2 mpr3 mpr4  │ none                     │
  ├────────────┼─────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ amharic    │ mpr1            │ none                     │
  ├────────────┼─────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ amharic    │ mpr2..mpr6      │ none                     │
  ├────────────┼─────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ sena       │ mpr1 mpr2 mpr3  │ none                     │
  └────────────┴─────────────────┴──────────────────────────┘

  excludedMPRFeatures and ruleFeatures are reads — they can't cause a drop. So
  five groups are declared and never written, and the sixth is a singleton,
  where to_remove = members & !output is provably empty.

  The caveat: this is a degenerate discharge, not a solved construct. We have
  been refusing all three reference grammars for a construct they declare but 
  never use. That's a genuine over-refusal and worth fixing — but Construction 2
  clears them via "nothing writes to the group," not via its interesting case.
  A grammar that actually co-occurs two members of one Overwrite group remains
  uncompilable, and per the standing rule that sample numbers may motivate
  research but never narrow a design, "all three reference grammars pass" must
  not become the bar. The predicate has to handle real co-occurrence, and a
  stress grammar has to exercise an actually-dropping group.

  Incidentally this corrects model.rs:927-948's own doc comment, which
  attributes the unobservability to singleton groups. Right conclusion, wrong
  mechanism — it's dead declarations. And Amharic and Sena declaring six and
  three entirely-unused MPR features is a third instance of the
  declared-unreachable-tag pattern from task #20.

  The flag-diacritic answer is the useful negative result. It had already been
  tried in this codebase for this exact purpose — pg-foma/src/gate.rs's module
  doc records a prototype hitting three toolkit failures, including a crash when
  a flag sits inside a -> replace rule's context, which is precisely where the
  one real MPR usage lives. The agent reproduced the fsm_compose collapse
  against vendored foma=0.4.2 and confirmed fsm_intersect has no flag-awareness
  at all. flag_eliminate is sound and survives intersect, but is inaccessible
  for phonological rewrite rules. That closes the branch properly rather than
  leaving it as a maybe — and it's the answer to your "X and not X" question:
  the inverse-label encoding exists (dual-rail, Construction 3, sound with
  admit-both-on-contradiction) but costs a 4^k per-group state multiplier, and
  no grammar we have needs it.

  It also lands directly on the skill design: this was trigger (e), and the 
  answer came from reachability analysis, not a clever encoding. That's the
  first evidence for the acceptance test I proposed — the process has to be able
  to produce "check whether the bad case is even reachable" before "invent a
  lattice."

  Two questions from my last message are still open, and they're the ones that
  fix the skill's shape: structural counts as the gate with wall-clock tracked 
  but not gating — is that read right? And is "broadly useful" a scoring
  tiebreaker that a big single-grammar win can outvote, or a hard veto?
