● That reframes it correctly, and it kills my own framing on the question I
  asked. Let me fix the tasks and then answer properly.

● Your "20–50 nodes, one-offs are fine, register what worked" answers the
  question I asked better than either option I offered. Generality isn't a gate
  or a veto — it's harvested afterward. A veto would have blocked exactly the
  one-offs you want. Dropping that from the design.

  Four things follow, and one is a hard architectural constraint I should flag
  now.

  The closed-enum problem — this is the real design decision

  plan.rs has 5 closed node kinds (Leaf, Compose, Union, Gate, Replace), and
  that closure is deliberate: ADR 0002 content-addresses every node with FNV-1a
  to get Merkle identity and plan caching. 20–50 constructs cannot each be a new
  enum variant — that's 20–50 ADR-level changes, and each one invalidates every
  cached plan hash.

  So a "node" in your sense has to be a named, parameterized recipe over the 
  existing five — a plan-shape template, plus an admitting predicate, plus its
  measured evidence. The registry then grows without bound while the enum stays
  closed and content-addressing keeps working. That also makes "usable in
  combination" fall out naturally, since recipes compose as subtrees rather than
  needing to know about each other.

  If that's wrong — if you'd rather the enum genuinely open up — it's worth
  saying now, because it's an ADR 0002 amendment and it changes the plan cache.

  Your "possibility space" framing has a precise technical counterpart

  What you're describing — a speaker navigating interwoven rules without effort
  — is a factorization of the possibility space whose combinatorics stay 
  bounded. The graph-theoretic name for "how much of the space must be held
  simultaneously" is treewidth. I'd flagged it as one metric among many in the
  research brief; your framing makes it the headline, because it's the quantity
  that distinguishes "this grammar's rules interweave cheaply" from "they
  don't." A low-treewidth factorization is an efficient realization of the
  possibility space. That's the thread I'd pull hardest on.

  The registry's hard part is selection, not collection

  Fifty nodes with no selection predicate is worse than five with them — that's
  how you get a bag of tricks nobody can choose from. So every entry needs the
  shape it admits stated as something checkable, not just "worked on an
  agglutinative grammar." The good news is this repo already has the mechanism:
  characterize() predicates are exactly that, and ConfigPredicate verdicts are
  already per-configuration rather than per-grammar.

  The registry must hold negative results too — and today proved it

  "Tried here, failed for this reason" is worth as much as a success, and we
  just got direct evidence. The MPR research closed the flag-diacritic branch in
  hours instead of re-prototyping it, because gate.rs's module doc had recorded
  a prior failed attempt — a crash when a flag sits inside a -> replace rule's
  context, which is precisely where the construct would have needed to live.
  That negative entry was worth days. It's also the same shape as
  dead-end-census's E1/E3/E4 being parked build-ready, not dead — verdicts
  per-corpus, not permanent.

  On structural counts as the gate with wall-clock tracked-not-gating, I'll take
  your wording as agreement and build it that way.

  decay into an auction. That's the right call and it fits your registry framing
  better than a weighted score would — Pareto keeps one-off wins visible
  instead of averaging them away.

  construct, never gated. Negative results are first-class entries — today's
  flag-diacritic branch closed in hours because gate.rs had recorded a prior
  failure, which is the clearest possible evidence for that. Scoring is Pareto,
  not weighted, so a narrow win stays visible.

  Settled by measurement: three of your five triggers can't currently fire, so
  banding comes before the skill is usable. And lazy composition is the obvious
  first registry entry — the decision was already made and recorded in the enum;
  only the builder is missing.

  Still blocking, and it's yours to call: the closed-enum question. plan.rs's
  five node kinds are closed by ADR 0002 design so that FNV-1a content
  addressing gives Merkle identity and plan caching. Twenty to fifty constructs
  can't each be a variant — that's an ADR amendment per construct, and each one
  invalidates every cached plan hash. My assumption is that a "node" in your
  sense becomes a named parameterized recipe over the existing five kinds —
  plan-shape template, admitting predicate, measured evidence — which keeps the
  enum closed, keeps content-addressing working, and makes "usable in
  combination" fall out because recipes compose as subtrees.

  That's the assumption I'll build on if you don't say otherwise. I'm flagging
  it rather than just proceeding because if you'd rather the enum genuinely open
  up, that's a different architecture and it's much cheaper to decide now than
  after ten registry entries exist.

  One correction to something I said earlier in this session: I framed the
  generality weighting as tiebreaker-versus-veto and asked you to pick. Both
  were wrong — a veto would have blocked precisely the one-offs you're after.

● Let me read the actual definitions rather than paraphrase from memory — this
  is worth getting exactly right.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)
● Good — this is the thing worth being clear on, because everything else depends
  on it.

  What a "plan" actually is

  It's not the FST. It's the recipe for building the FST — a DAG where leaves
  are compilable source fragments and interior nodes are operations that combine
  them. plan.rs has exactly five verbs:

  ┌─────────┬──────────────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────┐
  │  Node   │             What it does             │     Effect on size     │
  ├─────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┤
  │         │ compile one fragment (a lexicon      │                        │
  │ Leaf    │ slice, one rewrite rule, a guard     │ base cost              │
  │         │ automaton)                           │                        │
  ├─────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┤
  │ Compose │ run one FST into another             │ multiplies — up to     │
  │         │                                      │ |A|×|B| states         │
  ├─────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┤
  │ Union   │ merge independent branches           │ adds — |A|+|B|         │
  ├─────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┤
  │         │ split the input space by a key,      │                        │
  │ Gate    │ build a smaller net per part, union  │ turns a × into a +     │
  │         │ them                                 │                        │
  ├─────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┤
  │ Replace │ an ordered rewrite cascade           │ multiplies, and can't  │
  │         │                                      │ be freely reordered    │
  └─────────┴──────────────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────┘

  That table is the whole game. Compose multiplies and Union adds. Everything
  you're calling "smashing them so the combinatorics don't explode" is: arrange 
  the tree so the multiplications happen over small factors and the additions 
  happen over big ones.

  Why the features exploding is the default, not the exception

  Say a grammar has 5 parts of speech, 6 MPR features, and 3 strata. A single
  monolithic FST has to be able to represent every combination it can't rule
  out: 5 × 2⁶ × 3 = 960 distinct feature states, each dragging its own copy of
  the network structure.

  But — and this is exactly your point about a speaker — almost none of those 
  960 co-occur in the real language. The actual possibility space is sparse and
  correlated: certain MPR features only ever appear on verbs, certain strata
  never see certain features. A speaker never navigates the product; they
  navigate the sparse reachable subset without thinking about it.

  Naive compilation builds the product. A good tree builds the sparse subset.
  That gap is where the 100× wins live.

  Same grammar, different trees, wildly different cost

  Concretely, two ways to handle POS-conditioned subrules:

  (A)  Compose[ lexicon , allSubrules ]          →  |lex| × |subrules|
  multiplication
  (B)  Gate[ by POS: Compose[lex_noun, subrules_noun]
                   , Compose[lex_verb, subrules_verb] ... ]   →  Σ small
  products   addition of small multiplications

  (B) is what gate.rs already does — partition lexical entries by which subrules
  they're eligible for, compile a smaller net per group, union. Both trees
  compute the same language. One fits on a phone.

  Which one wins depends on how the features actually correlate in that grammar.
  If POS cleanly predicts subrule eligibility, (B) is a huge win. If every POS
  can take every subrule, (B) is pure overhead — you've duplicated the subrules
  with no reduction. That's why there's no single best tree, and why you're 
  right that you want 20–50 of them. An agglutinative grammar with independent
  slots factors cleanly by slot. A templatic one doesn't, because its slots
  interlock — the thing that makes it templatic is exactly that its features
  don't factor.

  Your Aweti case is the sharpest in-repo example: 41 zero-width truncation
  rules × a 24-level derivation chain, which OOM'd. The P6 templated emitter was
  a different tree for the same grammar, and it emits in under 3 seconds.
  Nothing about the grammar changed.

  Why this means 20–50 nodes without 20–50 enum variants

  ADR 0002's lineage is Volcano/Cascades — database query planning. That analogy
  is exact and clarifying: a query planner has a tiny closed set of operators
  (scan, join, union, filter) but an unbounded space of query plans. What a good
  planner accumulates isn't new operators, it's known-good strategies plus the 
  condition for picking each one — hash join when one side is small, merge join
  when both are sorted, nested loop when the inner is indexed.

  Your registry is that. A "node" in your sense is a named recipe: "when a 
  grammar's features correlate like this, arrange Gate/Compose/Union like that, 
  and here's what it measured." The five verbs are the closed set; the sentences
  you write with them are unbounded. Which is why the closed enum isn't
  actually a constraint on your 20–50 — it's what makes them composable, because
  recipes plug into each other as subtrees without needing to know about one
  another.

  The one piece the enum genuinely does gate is a new kind of operation —
  something that's neither a compose, a union, a gate, nor a cascade. Lazy
  composition isn't one of those (it's already a strategy field on Compose,
  deliberately kept separate from topology so cost choices don't multiply the
  topology space). If a registry entry ever does need a genuinely sixth verb,
  that's an ADR amendment — but I'd expect that to be rare, and the honest test
  is whether it can be expressed as an arrangement of the five first.

  So the earlier question I flagged as blocking is mostly answered by the
  analogy itself: recipes over five verbs. The part still worth your call is
  whether a recipe should be a first-class stored object the planner can
  enumerate over automatically, or documentation an engineer follows by hand.
  The first is much more powerful and much more work; the second is where I'd
  start, because a registry of hand-followed recipes with honest measurements is
  still the compounding asset, and it tells us which recipes deserve
  automating.
